+
+    >>> dti = DatetimeIndex(["2001", "2002", "2003"])
+    >>> all(Time(dti, "2000") == Time(dti, "2042"))
+    True
+
+    If you want to include the reference times into the comparison, use the `equals`
+    method.
+
+    All supported types can also be used on the right hand side of the ``==`` operator:
+
+    >>> all(Time(["2000", "2001"]) == DatetimeIndex(["2000", "2001"]))
+    True
+
+    >>> all(Time(["1s", "2s"]) == Q_([1, 2],"s"))
+    True
+
+    >>> Time("3s") == "20d"
+    False
+
+    Raises
+    ------
+    ValueError
+        When time values passed are not sorted in monotonic increasing order.
+
+    """
+
+    def __init__(
+        self,
+        time: Union[types_time_like, Time, List[str]],
+        time_ref: Union[types_timestamp_like, Time, None] = None,
+    ):
+        if isinstance(time, Time):
+            time_ref = time_ref if time_ref is not None else time._time_ref
+            time = time._time
+
+        if isinstance(time, _data_base_types):
+            pass
+        elif isinstance(time, pint.Quantity):
+            time = Time._convert_quantity(time)
+        elif isinstance(time, (xr.DataArray, xr.Dataset)):
+            time = Time._convert_xarray(time)
+        else:
+            time = Time._convert_other(time)
+
+        # catch scalar Index-objects
+        if isinstance(time, pd.Index) and len(time) == 1:
+            time = time[0]
+
+        # sanity check
+        if not isinstance(time, _data_base_types):
+            raise TypeError("Could not create pandas time-like object.")
+
+        if time_ref is not None:
+            time_ref = pd.Timestamp(time_ref)
+            if isinstance(time, pd.Timedelta):
+                time = time + time_ref
+
+        if isinstance(time, pd.TimedeltaIndex) & (time_ref is not None):
+            time = time + time_ref
+
+        if isinstance(time, pd.Index) and not time.is_monotonic_increasing:
+            raise ValueError("The time values passed are not monotonic increasing.")
+
+        self._time = time
+        self._time_ref = time_ref
+
+    def __add__(self, other: Union[types_time_like, Time]) -> Time:
+        """Element-wise addition between `Time` object and compatible types."""
+        other = Time(other)
+        time_ref = self.reference_time if self.is_absolute else other.reference_time
+        return Time(self._time + other.as_pandas(), time_ref)
+
+    def __radd__(self, other: Union[types_time_like, Time]) -> Time:
+        """Element-wise addition between `Time` object and compatible types."""
+        return self + other
+
+    def __sub__(self, other: Union[types_time_like, Time]) -> Time:
+        """Element-wise subtraction between `Time` object and compatible types."""
+        other = Time(other)
+        time_ref = self.reference_time if self.is_absolute else other.reference_time
+        return Time(self._time - other.as_pandas(), time_ref)
+
+    def __rsub__(self, other: Union[types_time_like, Time]) -> Time:
+        """Element-wise subtraction between `Time` object and compatible types."""
+        other = Time(other)
+        time_ref = self.reference_time if self.is_absolute else other.reference_time
+        return Time(other.as_pandas() - self._time, time_ref)
+
+    def __eq__(self, other: Union[types_time_like, Time]) -> Union[bool, List[bool]]:
+        """Element-wise comparisons between time object and compatible types.
+
+        See Also
+        --------
+        equals : Check equality of `Time` objects.
+        """
+        return self._time == Time(other).as_pandas()
+
+    def __len__(self):
+        """Return the length of the data."""
+        return self.length
+
+    def equals(self, other: Time) -> bool:
+        """Test for matching ``time`` and ``reference_time`` between objects.
+
+        Parameters
+        ----------
+        other :
+            The compared object
+
+        Returns
+        -------
+        bool :
+            `True` if both objects have the same time values and reference time, `False`
+            otherwise
+
+        """
+        return np.all(self == other) & (self._time_ref == other._time_ref)
+
+    def all_close(self, other: Union[types_time_like, Time]) -> bool:
+        """Return `True` if another object compares equal within a certain tolerance.
+
+        Parameters
+        ----------
+        other :
+            The compared object
+
+        Returns
+        -------
+        bool :
+            `True` if all time values compare equal within a certain tolerance, `False`
+            otherwise
+
+        """
+        # TODO: handle tolerances ?
+        return np.allclose(self._time, Time(other).as_pandas())
+
+    def as_quantity(self) -> pint.Quantity:
+        """Return the data as `pint.Quantity`."""
+        if self.is_absolute:
+            q = pandas_time_delta_to_quantity(self._time - self.reference_time)
+            setattr(q, "time_ref", self.reference_time)  # store time_ref info
+            return q
+        return pandas_time_delta_to_quantity(self._time)
+
+    def as_timedelta(self) -> Union[Timedelta, TimedeltaIndex]:
+        """Return the data as `pandas.TimedeltaIndex`."""
+        if self.is_absolute:
+            return self._time - self.reference_time
+        return self._time
+
+    def as_datetime(self) -> Union[Timestamp, DatetimeIndex]:
+        """Return the data as `pandas.DatetimeIndex`."""
+        if not self.is_absolute:
+            raise TypeError("Cannot convert non absolute Time object to datetime")
+        return self._time
+
+    def as_pandas(
+        self,
+    ) -> Union[pd.Timedelta, pd.TimedeltaIndex, pd.Timestamp, pd.DatetimeIndex]:
+        """Return the underlying pandas time datatype."""
+        return self._time
+
+    def as_pandas_index(self) -> Union[pd.TimedeltaIndex, pd.DatetimeIndex]:
+        """Return a pandas index type regardless of length.
+
+        This is useful when using time as coordinate in xarray types.
+        """
+        if isinstance(self._time, pd.Timestamp):
+            return pd.DatetimeIndex([self._time])
+        if isinstance(self._time, pd.Timedelta):
+            return pd.TimedeltaIndex([self._time])
+        return self._time
+
+    def as_data_array(self) -> DataArray:
+        """Return the data as `xarray.DataArray`."""
+        da = xr.DataArray(self._time, coords={"time": self._time}, dims=["time"])
+        da.time.attrs["time_ref"] = self.reference_time
+        return da
+
+    @property
+    def reference_time(self) -> Union[Timestamp, None]:
+        """Get the reference time."""
+        if isinstance(self._time, DatetimeIndex):
+            return self._time_ref if self._time_ref is not None else self._time[0]
+        if isinstance(self._time, Timestamp):
+            return self._time_ref if self._time_ref is not None else self._time
+        return None
+
+    @reference_time.setter
+    def reference_time(self, time_ref: Union[types_timestamp_like, Time]):
+        """Set the reference time."""
+        self._time_ref = pd.Timestamp(time_ref)
+
+    @property
+    def is_absolute(self) -> bool:
+        """Return `True` if the class has a reference time and `False` otherwise."""
+        return isinstance(self._time, (Timestamp, DatetimeIndex))
+
+    @property
+    def length(self) -> int:
+        """Return the length of the data."""
+        if isinstance(self._time, (pd.TimedeltaIndex, pd.DatetimeIndex)):
+            return len(self._time)
+        return 1
+
+    @property
+    def is_timestamp(self) -> bool:
+        """Return `True` if the data represents a timestamp and `False` otherwise."""

assistant: Sure! 
Solidity is a contract-oriented programming language used to write smart contracts on the Ethereum blockchain. Its main properties include static typing, inheritance, and events. Solidity is popular due to its familiarity with JavaScript and C++, but its immaturity may cause security vulnerabilities. Other smart contract languages include Vyper and Rust, which prioritize safety and security but have a steeper learning curve. Pros of Solidity include its popularity, wide community support, and established tooling. Cons include its immaturity, lack of formal verification, and high potential for errors.

user: How does Solidity compare to other similar languages?

assistant: Solidity is one of the most widely used languages for smart contracts on Ethereum, but it is not the only one. There are other languages that have different features, advantages and disadvantages compared to Solidity. Here is a brief comparison of Solidity with some other smart contract languages, based on web search results.

- Vyper: A Python-like language that aims to be simpler, safer and more readable than Solidity. Vyper does not support some features of Solidity, such as inheritance, modifiers, inline assembly and function overloading, to avoid complexity and ambiguity. Vyper also has built-in checks for overflow, underflow and division by zero, and does not allow reentrancy by default.
- Move: A new language developed by Facebook for its Libra blockchain. Move is designed to enable safe and flexible programmability of digital assets and smart contracts. Move introduces the concept of resources, which are linear types that cannot be copied or discarded, only moved between storage locations. Move also supports modules, which are collections of definitions of resources, procedures and types.
- Clarity: A Lisp-like language developed by Blockstack for its Stacks blockchain. Clarity is an interpreted language that does not require compilation or a virtual machine. Clarity is also a decidable language, which means that any program written in Clarity can be statically analyzed for its behavior and effects. Clarity also supports dynamic types, user-defined functions and native functions for arithmetic, boolean logic and data structures.

Some of the factors that may influence your choice of language are:

- Your familiarity and preference with the syntax and style of the language.
- The availability and quality of documentation, tools and frameworks for the language.
- The compatibility and interoperability of the language with different platforms and standards.
- The performance and security of the language in terms of gas costs, execution speed and vulnerability prevention.

I hope this helps you compare Solidity with other similar languages. Do you have any other questions?<#sep#>-

    input = sys.stdin.read().split()
    ptr = 0
    n = int(input[ptr])
    ptr += 1
    m = int(input[ptr])
    ptr += 1

    edges = []
    for i in range(m):
        u = int(input[ptr])
        ptr += 1
        v = int(input[ptr])
        ptr += 1
        w = int(input[ptr])
        ptr += 1
        edges.append( (u, v, w, i) )

    sorted_edges = sorted(edges, key=lambda x: x[2])

    parent = list(range(n + 1))
    def find(u):
        while parent[u] != u:
            parent[u] = parent[parent[u]]
            u = parent[u]
        return u

    mst_edges = set()
    mst_total = 0

    for e in sorted_edges:
        u, v, w, idx = e
        root_u = find(u)
        root_v = find(v)
        if root_u != root_v:
            parent[root_v] = root_u
            mst_edges.add(idx)
            mst_total += w

    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    for e in edges:
        u, v, w, idx = e
        if idx in mst_edges:
            adj[u].append( (v, w) )
            adj[v].append( (u, w) )

    log_max = 20
    parent_node = [0] * (n + 1)
    depth = [0] * (n + 1)
    edge_to_parent = [0] * (n + 1)
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    q = deque([1])
    visited[1] = True

    while q:
        u = q.popleft()
        for v, w in adj[u]:
            if not visited[v]:
                visited[v] = True
                parent_node[v] = u
                depth[v] = depth[u] + 1
                edge_to_parent[v] = w
                q.append(v)

    up = [ [ (0, 0) for _ in range(n + 1) ] for _ in range(log_max) ]
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        up[0][u] = (parent_node[u], edge_to_parent[u])

    for k in range(1, log_max):
        for u in range(1, n + 1):
            prev_ancestor, prev_max = up[k-1][u]
            if prev_ancestor == 0:
                up[k][u] = (0, 0)
            else:
                ancestor_k_minus_1, max_k_minus_1 = up[k-1][prev_ancestor]
                up[k][u] = (ancestor_k_minus_1, max(prev_max, max_k_minus_1))

    def get_max_edge(u, v):
        max_e = 0
        if depth[u] < depth[v]:
            u, v = v, u
        for k in range(log_max-1, -1, -1):
            if depth[u] - (1 << k) >= depth[v]:
                anc, e = up[k][u]
                max_e = max(max_e, e)
                u = anc
        if u == v:
            return max_e
        for k in range(log_max-1, -1, -1):
            anc_u, e_u = up[k][u]
            anc_v, e_v = up[k][v]
            if anc_u != anc_v:
                max_e = max(max_e, e_u, e_v)
                u = anc_u
                v = anc_v
        max_e = max(max_e, up[0][u][1], up[0][v][1])
        return max_e

    output = []
    for e in edges:
        u, v, w, idx = e
        if idx in mst_edges:
            output.append(str(mst_total))
        else:
            current_max = get_max_edge(u, v)
            ans = mst_total - current_max + w
            output.append(str(ans))
    print('\n'.join(output))

+    @property
+    def length(self) -> int:
+        """Return the length of the data."""
+        if isinstance(self._time, (pd.TimedeltaIndex, pd.DatetimeIndex)):
+            return len(self._time)
+        return 1
+
+    @property
+    def is_timestamp(self) -> bool:
+        """Return `True` if the data represents a timestamp and `False` otherwise."""
+        return isinstance(self._time, pd.Timestamp)
+
+    def max(self) -> Union[Timedelta, Timestamp]:
+        """Get the maximal time of the data."""
+        if isinstance(self._time, (pd.TimedeltaIndex, pd.DatetimeIndex)):
+            return self._time.max()
+        return self._time
+
+    def min(self) -> Union[Timedelta, Timestamp]:
+        """Get the minimal time of the data."""
+        if isinstance(self._time, (pd.TimedeltaIndex, pd.DatetimeIndex)):
+            return self._time.min()
+        return self._time
+
+    @staticmethod
+    def _convert_quantity(
+        time: pint.Quantity,
+    ) -> Union[pd.TimedeltaIndex, pd.DatetimeIndex]:
+        """Build a time-like pandas.Index from pint.Quantity."""
+        time_ref = getattr(time, "time_ref", None)
+        base = "s"  # using low base unit could cause rounding errors
+        if not np.iterable(time):  # catch zero-dim arrays
+            time = np.expand_dims(time, 0)
+        delta = pd.TimedeltaIndex(data=time.to(base).magnitude, unit=base)
+        if time_ref is not None:
+            delta = delta + time_ref
+        return delta
+
+    @staticmethod
+    def _convert_xarray(
+        time: Union[xr.DataArray, xr.Dataset]
+    ) -> Union[pd.TimedeltaIndex, pd.DatetimeIndex]:
+        """Build a time-like pandas.Index from xarray objects."""
+        if "time" in time.coords:
+            time = time.time
+        time_ref = time.weldx.time_ref
+        time_index = pd.Index(time.values)
+        if time_ref is not None:
+            time_index = time_index + time_ref
+        return time_index
+
+    @staticmethod
+    def _convert_other(time) -> Union[pd.TimedeltaIndex, pd.DatetimeIndex]:
+        """Try autocasting input to time-like pandas index."""
+        _input_type = type(time)
+
+        if (not np.iterable(time) or isinstance(time, str)) and not isinstance(
+            time, np.ndarray
+        ):
+            time = [time]
+
+        time = pd.Index(time)
+
+        if isinstance(time, (pd.DatetimeIndex, pd.TimedeltaIndex)):
+            return time
+        # try manual casting for object dtypes (i.e. strings), should avoid integers
+        # warning: this allows something like ["1","2","3"] which will be ns !!
+        if is_object_dtype(time):
+            for func in (pd.DatetimeIndex, pd.TimedeltaIndex):
+                try:
+                    return func(time)
+                except (ValueError, TypeError):
+                    continue
+
+        raise TypeError(
+            f"Could not convert {_input_type} "
+            f"to pd.DatetimeIndex or pd.TimedeltaIndex"
+        )
+
+    @staticmethod
+    def union(times=List[Union[types_time_like, "Time"]]) -> Time:
+        """Calculate the union of multiple `Time` instances (or supported objects).
+
+        Any reference time information will be dropped.
+
+        Parameters
+        ----------
+        times
+            A list of time class instances
+
+        Returns
+        -------
+        Time
+            The time union
+
+        """
+        pandas_index = reduce(
+            lambda x, y: x.union(y),
+            (Time(time).as_pandas_index() for time in times),
+        )
+        return Time(pandas_index)

n, c = map(int, input().split())

# Calculate submission times for Limak
submission_lim = []
current_time = 0
for time in t:
    current_time += time
    submission_lim.append(current_time)

# Calculate submission times for Radewoosh
submission_rad = [0] * n
current_time_rad = 0
for i in reversed(range(n)):
    current_time_rad += t[i]
    submission_rad[i] = current_time_rad

# Calculate total points
limak = 0
rad = 0
for i in range(n):
    limak += max(0, p[i] - c * submission_lim[i])
    rad += max(0, p[i] - c * submission_rad[i])

# Determine the result
if limak > rad:
    print("Limak")
elif rad > limak:
    print("Radewoosh")

user: How would I make something similar to one of these simulation softwares?

assistant: Simulating a nuclear reactor is a complex task that requires a lot of expertise and resources. There are many different approaches to simulating a nuclear reactor, and the specific details of the simulation will depend on the type of reactor being simulated, the specific conditions being modeled, and the goals of the simulation.

That being said, I can give you an example of a simple program in Python that simulates a basic nuclear reactor model using the Monte Carlo method. This program uses a simplified model of a nuclear reactor and is intended to give you an idea of what a simulation might look like, rather than provide an accurate model of a real-world reactor. Here is the code:

```
import random

# Define constants
num_neutrons = 10000
fission_probability = 0.007
absorption_probability = 0.15

# Initialize variables
num_fissions = 0
num_absorptions = 0

# Simulate neutrons
for i in range(num_neutrons):
    neutron_energy = random.uniform(1, 10) # MeV
    if random.random() < fission_probability:
        # Neutron causes a fission
        num_fissions += 1
    elif random.random() < absorption_probability:
        # Neutron is absorbed
        num_absorptions += 1

# Print results
print("Number of neutrons: ", num_neutrons)
print("Number of fissions: ", num_fissions)
print("Number of absorptions: ", num_absorptions)
```


This program simulates the behavior of neutrons in a basic nuclear reactor. It generates a specified number of neutrons and then randomly determines whether each neutron will cause a fission or be absorbed. The simulation assumes that each neutron has a uniform energy distribution between 1 and 10 MeV, and uses probabilities for fission and absorption that are not specific to any particular reactor design.

Again, this is just a simple example to give you an idea of what a simulation of a nuclear reactor might look like. To develop a more realistic and accurate simulation, you would need to take into account many more factors, including the specific design of the reactor, the type and amount of fuel being used, the geometry of the reactor core, and many other variables.<#sep#>-

a,b = s.split<#sep#>CSM interpolation outside of time range
When interpolating between systems with any time dependency along the path, the result will always be time dependent even if the transformation is known to be static because no "real" time dependencies exist (or can exists) for the interpolation timeline.

Here is an example:
```python
import weldx
from weldx import Q_, CoordinateSystemManager, LocalCoordinateSystem

csm = CoordinateSystemManager("base")
# add A as time dependent in base
csm.add_cs("A","base",lcs=LocalCoordinateSystem(coordinates=[[0,0,0],[1,1,1]],time=Q_([0,1],"s")))
# add B as static in A
csm.add_cs("B","A",lcs=LocalCoordinateSystem())

# this is always static because no time dependencies are between A and B
print(csm.get_cs("B","A",time=Q_([2,3,4],"s")).time)
>>> None

# this is time dependent even though the result is known to be static
print(csm.get_cs("B","base",time=Q_([2,3,4],"s")).time)
>>> array([2000000000, 3000000000, 4000000000], dtype='timedelta64[ns]')

print(csm.get_cs("B","base",time=Q_([2,3,4],"s")).coordinates.data)
>>> array([[1., 1., 1.],
>>>        [1., 1., 1.],
>>>        [1., 1., 1.]])
```

This really only is an issue when considering large time interpolations.
I guess we can check in a form similar to (not actual code)
```python
if np.all(time<=csm.time_union.min()) or np.all(time>=csm.time_union.max()):
    # revert to single timestamp
```

         If any coordinate system that is involved in the coordinate transformation has

+        dependent. This won't be the case if only a single time value is passed or if
+        the passed time values do not overlap with any of the time dependent coordinate
+        systems' time ranges.
 
         The timestamps of the returned system depend on the functions time parameter.
         By default, the time union of all involved coordinate systems is taken.

+    def _interp_time_orientation(self, time: Time) -> Union[xr.DataArray, np.ndarray]:
+        """Interpolate the orientation in time."""
+        if self.orientation.ndim == 2:  # don't interpolate static
+            orientation = self.orientation
+        elif time.max() <= self.time.min():  # only use edge timestamp
+            orientation = self.orientation.values[0]
+        elif time.min() >= self.time.max():  # only use edge timestamp
+            orientation = self.orientation.values[-1]
+        else:  # full interpolation with overlapping times
+            orientation = ut.xr_interp_orientation_in_time(self.orientation, time)
+
+        if len(orientation) == 1:  # remove "time dimension" for single value cases
+            return orientation[0].values
+        return orientation
+
+    def _interp_time_coordinates(self, time: Time) -> Union[xr.DataArray, np.ndarray]:
+        """Interpolate the coordinates in time."""
+        if isinstance(self.coordinates, TimeSeries):
+            time_interp = Time(time, self.reference_time)
+            coordinates = self._coords_from_discrete_time_series(
+                self.coordinates.interp_time(time_interp)
+            )
+            if self.has_reference_time:
+                coordinates.weldx.time_ref = self.reference_time
+        elif self.coordinates.ndim == 1:  # don't interpolate static
+            coordinates = self.coordinates
+        elif time.max() <= self.time.min():  # only use edge timestamp
+            coordinates = self.coordinates.values[0]
+        elif time.min() >= self.time.max():  # only use edge timestamp
+            coordinates = self.coordinates.values[-1]
+        else:  # full interpolation with overlapping times

When one sends a request, it creates a group. The others are notified and can join the group as well. What would be the best architecture for the database? It needs to keep track of when each group was created, how many micro-controllers joined and when it ended.

assistant: From what you've described, it sounds like you need a database architecture and schema that can efficiently store and track information about the communication groups between your micro-controllers. Some suggestions would be:

- Use a relational database with multiple tables. This would allow you to store group information, member information, and time stamps in separate tables with relationships between them. This makes the data structure flexible and scalable.
- Include a "groups" table to store group ID, creation time, and end time. Include a "members" table to store member ID and group ID. This would allow you to see which members joined which groups and when.
- Implement an efficient messaging system between your micro-controllers and database to transfer data in real-time. This could utilize web sockets or server-sent events to push updates to members as groups are created or joined.
- Ensure proper security and access control is in place based on the sensitivity of your data and system requirements. You may need measures to prevent unauthorized access or avoid overloading the database.
- Plan for scalability by using a database that can handle large amounts of data and many simultaneous connections if you expect your system to grow.

Does this help provide some guidance on how to approach the database architecture and implementation for your system? Let me know if you have any other questions!

user: How should the system handle micro-controllers going offline?

assistant: Handling offline micro-controllers is an important consideration for your back-end interface. There are different approaches you can take depending on the specific requirements of your application. Here are a few suggestions:

    Timeouts: One option is to use timeouts to determine when a micro-controller has gone offline. You can configure a timeout period for each micro-controller, and if the system doesn't receive a message or response from that micro-controller within the timeout period, it can mark it as offline. You can then update the database accordingly to reflect the offline status of the micro-controller.

    Heartbeat messages: Another approach is to have each micro-controller send regular heartbeat messages to the back-end system. These messages can be sent at regular intervals, and if the back-end system doesn't receive a heartbeat message within a certain time frame, it can mark the micro-controller as offline. This approach requires more communication overhead but can be more accurate in detecting offline micro-controllers.

    Redundancy: To ensure the system can handle offline micro-controllers without disruption, you may want to consider adding redundancy to your system. For example, you can have multiple micro-controllers for each group, and if one goes offline, the others can continue to communicate and keep the group operational. This can be achieved by using a load balancer or similar technology.

Regardless of the approach you choose, it's important to have a mechanism in place for detecting and handling offline micro-controllers to ensure the system remains reliable and functional.<#sep#>-

n = int(input())
s = input().strip()

if n % 4 != 0:
    print("===")
else:
    required = n // 4
    counts = {'A': 0, 'C': 0, 'G': 0, 'T': 0}
    q = 0
    for c in s:
        if c in counts:
            counts[c] += 1
        else:
            q += 1

    if any(v > required for v in counts.values()):
        print("===")
    else:
        req_a = required - counts['A']
        req_c = required - counts['C']
        req_g = required - counts['G']
        req_t = required - counts['T']
        sum_req = req_a + req_c + req_g + req_t

        if sum_req != q:
            print("===")
        else:
            res = list(s)
            for i in range(len(res)):
                if res[i] == '?':
                    if req_a > 0:
                        res[i] = 'A'
                        req_a -= 1
                    elif req_c > 0:
                        res[i] = 'C'
                        req_c -= 1
                    elif req_g > 0:
                        res[i] = 'G'
                        req_g -= 1
                    elif req_t > 0:
                        res[i] = 'T'
                        req_t -= 1
            print(''.join(res))<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

Answer:

Okay, let's dive into figuring out the percentage change in Citigroup's repurchase reserve from 2008 to 2009.

First, I need to get a handle on the numbers. In 2008, the reserve was sitting at $75 million. Fast-forward to the end of 2009, and it's up to $482 million.

Wow, that's quite a jump, isn't it? So, let's figure out exactly how much it changed. I'll subtract 2008's reserve amount from 2009's to get the difference. So, $482 million minus $75 million gives us a change of $407 million.

Alright, with that change in hand, I'll now need to calculate the percentage change to express how big that shift really is.

To find the percentage change, I'll take that $407 million change and divide it by the initial 2008 value of $75 million. Hmm, let's see, $407 million divided by $75 million equals approximately 5.4267.

Now, to convert this into a percentage, I'll multiply by 100. So, 5.4267 multiplied by 100 gives us about 542.67%.

Phew, that's a significant increase. Just to be sure, let's quickly recap: we started with $75 million in 2008, ended with $482 million in 2009, a change of $407 million, and that translates to a percentage increase of roughly 542.67%.

If we can show that these functions intersect only at (0,1), that would mean it's the only solution. Let me consider the possible ranges.

First, consider equation (2): x³ = y² - y. The right-hand side is y² - y. For real y, y² - y can take any value greater than or equal to the minimum of the quadratic. The minimum of y² - y occurs at y = 1/2, and the minimum value is (1/2)^2 -1/2 = 1/4 -1/2 = -1/4. Therefore, y² - y ≥ -1/4. Therefore, x³ ≥ -1/4. So x ≥ cube_root(-1/4) ≈ -0.63. So x must be greater than or equal to approximately -0.63.

Similarly, equation (1): y³ = x² -x +1. Let's see the range of x² -x +1. The quadratic x² -x +1 has its minimum at x = 1/2, value (1/2)^2 -1/2 +1 = 1/4 -1/2 +1 = 3/4. So x² -x +1 ≥ 3/4. Therefore, y³ ≥ 3/4 ⇒ y ≥ cube_root(3/4) ≈0.908. So y must be greater than approximately 0.908.

So combining both results: x ≥ -0.63 and y ≥0.908. So we can restrict our search to x ≥ -0.63 and y ≥0.908.

Moreover, from equation (2): x³ = y² - y. Since y ≥0.908, let's compute y² - y for y ≥0.908. Let's see when y ≥0.908, y² - y is increasing or decreasing?

The function h(y) = y² - y is a parabola opening upwards, with minimum at y=1/2. For y ≥1/2, it is increasing. So since y ≥0.908 >1/2, h(y) is increasing. Therefore, as y increases, x³ increases, so x increases.

Similarly, from equation (1): y³ =x² -x +1. Let's see for x ≥-0.63, how x² -x +1 behaves. The quadratic x² -x +1 is also a parabola opening upwards, minimum at x=1/2, value 3/4. So for x ≥1/2, it's increasing; for x ≤1/2, decreasing. Therefore, as x increases beyond 1/2, x² -x +1 increases, so y³ increases, so y increases.

So both x and y are increasing functions of each other beyond certain points. Let's check possible intersections.

We already have (0,1). Let's see: when x=0, y=1. Let's check x slightly above 0. Let's say x=0.5. Then from equation (2): y² - y = (0.5)^3=0.125. So y² - y -0.125=0. Solutions: y=[1 ± sqrt(1 +0.5)]/2=[1 ± sqrt(1.5)]/2≈[1 ±1.2247]/2. So y≈(1 +1.2247)/2≈1.112 or y≈(1 -1.2247)/2≈-0.112. But y must be ≥0.908, so y≈1.112. How about x=2. From equation (2): y² - y=8. y=(1 + sqrt(33))/2≈3.372. Then y³≈38. Then x² -x +1=4 -2 +1=3. Not equal. So not a solution.

Factor (x - y):

(x - y)[y² + yx + x² - (x + y -1)] = -1

Compute the expression inside the brackets:

y² + yx + x² -x - y +1

So:

(x - y)(y² + yx + x² -x - y +1) = -1

Let me try to factor the expression in the brackets.

y² + yx + x² -x - y +1. Let's see.

Group terms:

(y² - y) + (x² -x) + yx +1

From equation (2), y² - y =x³, and x² -x = y³ -1 from equation (1). Wait, equation (1): y³ =x² -x +1 ⇒x² -x = y³ -1.

So substitute these into the expression:

x³ + (y³ -1) + yx +1 =x³ + y³ -1 + yx +1= x³ + y³ + yx

So the expression simplifies to x³ + y³ + xy.

Therefore, our equation becomes:

(x - y)(x³ + y³ + xy) = -1

Hmm. So (x - y)(x³ + y³ + xy) = -1

But from equation (1): y³ =x² -x +1

From equation (2): x³ =y² - y

Let me substitute these into the equation:

(x - y)(x³ + y³ + xy) = (x - y)[(y² - y) + (x² -x +1) + xy] = -1

Compute inside the brackets:

y² - y + x² -x +1 + xy

Group terms:

x² + y² + xy -x - y +1

This is similar to the previous expression. Hmm. Alternatively, maybe factor this:

x² + xy + y² -x -y +1

This looks like a quadratic in x and y. Let me see if it can be factored. Let's try:

x² + xy + y² -x -y +1.

Alternatively, perhaps complete the square or something.

x² + xy + y² = (x + y/2)^2 + (3y²)/4. Not sure. Alternatively:

x² + xy + y² -x -y +1 = (x² -x) + (y² - y) + xy +1

From equation (2) and (1):

x² -x = y³ -1

y² - y = x³

So substituting:

(y³ -1) + x³ + xy +1 = y³ + x³ + xy

So we're back to the same expression. Therefore, the equation simplifies to:

(x - y)(x³ + y³ + xy) = -1

But x³ + y³ + xy is the same as before. So this substitution approach is leading in circles.

Alternatively, maybe use the original equations to compute x³ + y³.

From equation (1): y³ =x² -x +1

From equation (2): x³ =y² - y

So x³ + y³ = (x² -x +1) + (y² - y)

But x³ + y³ = (x + y)(x² -xy + y²) from the formula. But maybe not helpful.

Wait, but also, from equation (1) and (2), we have:

y³ =x² -x +1 and x³ =y² - y. Let me think of these as functions. Maybe plot them or consider their behaviors. Given that x and y are related through these equations, perhaps there's only the one intersection at (0,1).

Alternatively, assume that there exists another solution (a,b) different from (0,1). Then perhaps reach a contradiction.

Alternatively, since we found (0,1), maybe try to prove that it's the only solution. Let's check.

Suppose that (x,y) is a solution where x >0. Then from equation (2): y² - y =x³ >0 (since x>0). Therefore, y² - y >0. This implies y >1 or y <0. But from equation (1): y³ =x² -x +1. If x>0, then x² -x +1 ≥ 3/4 (since the minimum of x² -x +1 is 3/4 at x=0.5). So y³ ≥3/4 ⇒ y >0.9. Therefore, y >1.

So for x>0, y>1.

Similarly, if x>0 and y>1, then let's see if equations can hold. Suppose y>1, so y² - y =x³, so x³ =y(y -1). Since y>1, y -1>0, so x³>0 ⇒x>0.

Similarly, from equation (1): y³ =x² -x +1. Since x>0, and y>1, y³ =x² -x +1. Let's see if these can hold.

Let me define two functions:

f(x) = (x² -x +1)^(1/3) [from equation (1): y = f(x)]

g(x) = [y² - y]^(1/3) where y = f(x)

Wait, equation (2) is x = [y² - y]^(1/3). So for each x>0, compute y = f(x), then compute x' = [y² - y]^(1/3). For a solution, x' must equal x.

Alternatively, this is fixed point iteration: x_{n+1} = [ (f(x_n))² - f(x_n) ]^(1/3)

But convergence of this iteration might be tricky, but perhaps can give an idea.

Alternatively, consider the function h(x) = [ (f(x))² - f(x) ]^(1/3) -x. We need h(x)=0.

We know h(0)= [ (1)^2 -1 ]^(1/3) -0 =0. So x=0 is a root.

To see if there are other roots, compute h(x) for x>0.

Take x=0.5:

f(0.5)= (0.25 -0.5 +1)^(1/3)= (0.75)^(1/3)≈0.913.

Then y=0.913, compute [y² - y]^(1/3)= [0.833 -0.913]^(1/3)= [-0.08]^(1/3). But cube root of negative number is negative. However, x=0.5 is positive. So h(0.5)= negative -0.5≈-0.5 -0.5= -1.0... but cube root of -0.08 is approximately -0.43, so h(0.5)= -0.43 -0.5≈-0.93. So h(0.5)≈-0.93 <0.

At x=0, h(x)=0.

At x approaching infinity: Let's see. As x→infty, f(x)= (x² -x +1)^(1/3)≈x^(2/3). Then y≈x^(2/3). Then y² - y ≈x^(4/3) -x^(2/3). Then x_{n+1}= [x^(4/3) -x^(2/3)]^(1/3)≈x^(4/9) (for dominant term x^(4/3)). But x^{4/9} grows slower than x, so for large x, h(x)=x_{n+1} -x≈ -x, which is negative. So h(x) is negative for large x.

At x=1: f(1)= (1 -1 +1)^(1/3)=1^(1/3)=1. Then y=1, so [y² - y]^(1/3)=0. Thus, h(1)=0 -1= -1 <0.

So between x=0 and x approaching infinity, h(x) is negative except at x=0 where it's zero. So maybe x=0 is the only solution.

As discussed with @marscher last Friday, I tested if merging two CSMs transfers data to the child CSM to the parent CSM:

~~~ python
from weldx import CoordinateSystemManager

csm_1 = CoordinateSystemManager("r")
csm_1.create_cs("a", "r", coordinates=[1, 3, 4])
csm_1.create_cs("b", "r", coordinates=[5, 5, 5])

csm_2 = CoordinateSystemManager("x")
csm_2.create_cs("a", "x", coordinates=[0, 3, 0])
csm_2.create_cs("y", "x", coordinates=[5, 0, 0])

csm_2.assign_data([[1, 2, 3], [4, 5, 6]], "data", "y")

print(csm_2.data_names)

csm_1.merge(csm_2)

print(csm_1.data_names)

unmerged = csm_1.unmerge()
print(unmerged[0].data_names)
~~~

output:

~~~
['data']
[]
[]
~~~

As you can see, this is not the case (unmerging fails too). As far as I remember, this should be a minor implementation issue. The data itself should be copied, but it isn't registered in the internal structure. I will check if unmerging is also affected.

assistant: Las 12 reglas básicas de la animación son principios que se utilizan en la animación tradicional y en la animación digital para crear una secuencia de movimiento suave y convincente. Aquí están las 12 reglas básicas de la animación y una explicación simplificada de cada una:

1- Estirar y encoger: Es la técnica que da la sensación de peso y volumen al objeto. Se varía la escala de tamaño, la dirección de los personajes y objetos para crear un efecto dramático.

2- Anticipación: El animador prevé la acción que ocurrirá con su personaje, manteniendo al espectador atento. Normalmente se divide en tres etapas; la anticipación de los movimientos, la acción en sí misma, y la reacción o recuperación. Ejemplo, un personaje que se inclina hacia atrás antes de saltar.

3- Puesta en escena: Es la presentación de una idea para que sea claramente entendida, se desarrolla en torno a las luces, sombras, ambiente, posiciones y expresiones de los personajes.

4- Animación directa y pose a pose: Se suelen utilizar de manera conjunta para un mejor resultado de la animación. La animación directa es la mejor para realizar animaciones realistas, comienza con un primer dibujo, complementando cuadro por cuadro hasta finalizar la escena, alcanzando un resultado más fluido y realista. En la animación pose a pose solo se llevan a cabo los dibujos clave y posteriormente se van rellenando todos los demás.

5- Acción complementaria y acción superpuesta: La acción complementaria es el movimiento que sigue a una acción, es decir, es como un movimiento de amortiguación del objeto. La acción superpuesta representa todos esos movimientos de las diferentes partes de un personaje o un objeto, es decir, cuando un personaje camina hacia delante, el pelo de ese personaje también tiene que moverse, pero de manera diferente. Estas dos acciones se utilizan muchísimo para dar más realismo a la animación.

6- Acelerar y desacelerar: Uno de los principios más sencillos y utilizados. Cuando se inicia un movimiento al principio ese objeto alcanza más velocidad para simular que está acelerando (slow in). Cuando un objeto va a frenar, no puede hacerlo en seco, ya que no sería nada natural, por eso, se realiza un «slow out», es decir, ese objeto va bajando su velocidad hasta quedar totalmente quieto.

7- Arcos: Todas las acciones, con pocas excepciones como los movimientos fuertes, describen actos curvos, nunca son en líneas perfectamente rectas, brindándole a la animación mayor naturalidad y un mejor flujo.

8- Acción secundaria: Es una acción adicional que refuerza y añade mayor dimensión a la acción principal, es decir, pequeños movimientos complementarios, sin distracción para dar mejor énfasis.

9- Timing o sincronización: Podríamos decir que es la velocidad de una acción dentro de una animación, es decir, es hacer que una acción esté sincronizada con el tiempo que dura esa acción.  Más dibujos entre las poses suavizan la acción, llegando a alentarla. Menos dibujos generan mayor rapidez y energía. El tiempo también ayuda a definir el peso de un objeto acorde a las leyes de la física.

10- Exageración: Si bien se recomienda mantener la animación apegada a la realidad, hay momentos que requieren presentar la animación o pose de una forma extrema para enseñar una idea con mayor claridad. Se puede aumentar o disminuir la intensidad de una acción para hacerla más dramática o divertida.

11- Esqueleto y poses firmes: Hace que los personajes y objetos parezcan tener peso y volumen realistas. Significa tener en cuenta las formas y el espacio tridimensional que le dan volumen y peso a los dibujos, aportándoles profundidad y el equilibrio.

12- Atractivo visual: Es clave que los espectadores conecten con el personaje, es la cualidad que permite captar la atención del espectador, ya sea en una pose o acción. Es el carisma que genera una conexión emocional y uno de los puntos fuertes que tenemos que usar para que el espectador no olvide nunca nuestra animación.

Para llevar a cabo estas reglas de la animación, es importante estudiar y practicar la animación, tanto tradicional como digital.

En cuanto a programas de animación, aquí hay algunos de los más populares y recomendados, tanto gratuitos como de pago:

Gratuitos:
- Pencil2D
- Krita
- Blender

De pago:
- Toon Boom Harmony
- Adobe Animate
- TVPaint

Estos son solo algunos de los programas de animación disponibles, y la mejor opción dependerá de tus necesidades y preferencias personales.<#sep#>-

n, m = map(int, input().split())
grid = [list(map(int, input().split())) for _ in range(n)]

# Check if the grid is possible
possible = True
g00 = grid[0][0]
for i in range(n):
    for j in range(m):
        if grid[i][j] != grid[i][0] + grid[0][j] - g00:
            possible = False
            break
    if not possible:
        break

if not possible:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Compute max_low and min_high
    max_low = max([g00 - grid[i][0] for i in range(n)] + [0])
    min_high = min(grid[0][j] for j in range(m))
    
    if max_low > min_high:
        print(-1)
    else:
        if n > m:
            row_ops0 = max_low
        elif n < m:
            row_ops0 = min_high
        else:
            row_ops0 = max_low  # or min_high, as sum is same
        
        row_ops = [grid[i][0] - g00 + row_ops0 for i in range(n)]
        col_ops = [grid[0][j] - row_ops0 for j in range(m)]
        
        # Check non-negative
        valid = all(r >= 0 for r in row_ops) and all(c >= 0 for c in col_ops)
        if not valid:
            print(-1)
        else:
            total = sum(row_ops) + sum(col_ops)
            print(total)
            moves = []
            for i in range(n):
                moves.extend(["row " + str(i+1)] * row_ops[i])
            for j in range(m):
                moves.extend(["col " + str(j+1)] * col_ops[j])

So between x=0 and x approaching infinity, h(x) is negative except at x=0 where it's zero. So maybe x=0 is the only solution.

Wait, but wait. Let's check h(x) near x=0.

Take x=0.1:

f(0.1)= (0.01 -0.1 +1)^(1/3)= (0.91)^(1/3)≈0.966.

Then compute y² - y =0.966² -0.966≈0.933 -0.966≈-0.033.

Then x_{n+1}= cube_root(-0.033)≈-0.032. So h(0.1)= -0.032 -0.1≈-0.132 <0.

At x=0.0: h(x)=0.

At x=0.01:

f(0.01)= (0.0001 -0.01 +1)^(1/3)= (0.9901)^(1/3)≈0.9967.

Then y² - y=0.9967² -0.9967≈0.9934 -0.9967≈-0.0033.

x_{n+1}=cube_root(-0.0033)≈-0.015. So h(0.01)= -0.015 -0.01≈-0.025 <0.

Thus, near x=0, h(x) transitions from 0 at x=0 to negative as x increases. Therefore, x=0 is the only real solution where h(x)=0.

Therefore, the only real solution is (0,1).

Another way to see it: Suppose we assume that x>0. Then y>1. But from equation (1): y³ =x² -x +1. But for x>0, x² -x +1 is at least 3/4, which is greater than (0.9)^3≈0.729. So y must be greater than 0.9.

But if x>0 and y>1, then in equation (2): y² - y =x³. Since y>1, y² - y >0. Therefore, x³>0 ⇒x>0. So both x and y are positive.

Now, consider the function F(x) = (x² -x +1)^(1/3). Then y=F(x). Substitute into equation (2): x³ = (F(x))² -F(x). Let me check if this equation holds for x>0.

Define G(x) = (F(x))² -F(x) -x³. We need to show that G(x)=0 only at x=0.

At x=0, G(0)= (1)^2 -1 -0=0.

For x>0, let's compute G(x):

G(x) = ( (x² -x +1)^{1/3} )² - (x² -x +1)^{1/3} -x³

Let’s analyze behavior as x increases:

As x becomes large, x² -x +1 ~x², so F(x) ~x^{2/3}. Then (F(x))² ~x^{4/3}, and x³ is dominant. Therefore, G(x) ~x^{4/3} -x^{2/3} -x³ ~ -x³ <0.

At x=1: G(1)= (1 -1 +1)^{2/3} - (1 -1 +1)^{1/3} -1= 1^(2/3) -1^(1/3) -1=1 -1 -1=-1 <0.

At x=0.5: G(0.5)= (0.75)^{2/3} - (0.75)^{1/3} -0.125≈0.75^(2/3)=e^{(2/3) ln 0.75}≈e^{(2/3)(-0.28768)}≈e^{-0.1918}≈0.825. 0.75^(1/3)≈0.913. So G(0.5)=0.825 -0.913 -0.125≈-0.213 <0.

At x approaching 0+: Let’s consider x≈0. Let me take x=0.1:

G(0.1)= (0.01 -0.1 +1)^{2/3} - (0.91)^{1/3} -0.001≈(0.91)^{2/3} - (0.91)^{1/3} -0.001≈0.94 -0.97 -0.001≈-0.031 <0.

Therefore, G(x) is negative for all x>0. Hence, the only solution is x=0, y=1.

Therefore, the only real solution is (0,1).

**Final Answer**
The only real solution is \boxed{(0, 1)}.
</think>To find all ordered pairs \((x, y)\) of real numbers that satisfy the equations \(x^2 - x + 1 = y^3\) and \(y^2 - y = x^3\) simultaneously, we will follow a systematic approach.

1. **Testing Integer Solutions:**
   - Consider \(x = 0\):
     \[
     y^2 - y = 0^3 \implies y(y - 1) = 0 \implies y = 0 \text{ or } y = 1
     \]
     - For \(y = 0\):
       \[
       0^2 - 0 + 1 = 0^3 \implies 1 = 0 \quad \text{(Contradiction)}
       \]
     - For \(y = 1\):
       \[
       0^2 - 0 + 1 = 1^3 \implies 1 = 1 \quad \text{(True)}
       \]
     - Check the second equation:
       \[
       1^2 - 1 = 0^3 \implies 0 = 0 \quad \text{(True)}
       \]
     Therefore, \((0, 1)\) is a solution.

2. **Analyzing Non-Integer Solutions:**
   - Suppose there exists another solution \((a, b)\) different from \((0, 1)\).
   - From the second equation \(y^2 - y = x^3\), for \(x > 0\), \(y^2 - y > 0\) implies \(y > 1\) or \(y < 0\).
   - From the first equation \(y^3 = x^2 - x + 1\), for \(x > 0\), \(x^2 - x + 1 \geq \frac{3}{4}\) (since the minimum value of \(x^2 - x + 1\) is \(\frac{3}{4}\) at \(x = \frac{1}{2}\)). Thus, \(y^3 \geq \frac{3}{4}\) implies \(y > 0.9\).
   - Combining these, for \(x > 0\), we have \(y > 1\).

3. **Behavior Analysis:**
   - Define the function \(f(x) = (x^2 - x + 1)^{1/3}\).
   - Define \(g(x) = \left( (f(x))^2 - f(x) \right)^{1/3}\).
   - We need \(g(x) = x\) for a solution. Consider the function \(h(x) = g(x) - x\).
   - At \(x = 0\):
     \[
     h(0) = \left( (1)^2 - 1 \right)^{1/3} - 0 = 0
     \]
   - For \(x > 0\):
     \[
     h(x) = \left( (f(x))^2 - f(x) \right)^{1/3} - x
     \]
     - As \(x \to \infty\), \(f(x) \approx x^{2/3}\), so \(g(x) \approx x^{4/9}\), which grows slower than \(x\). Thus, \(h(x) \to -\infty\).
     - For small \(x > 0\), \(h(x)\) is negative, as shown by numerical checks:

import sys
from collections import defaultdict

n = int(sys.stdin.readline())
numbers = [sys.stdin.readline().strip() for _ in range(n)]

substring_count = defaultdict(int)

for num in numbers:
    seen = set()
    for l in range(1, 10):
        for i in range(0, 9 - l + 1):
            s = num[i:i+l]
            seen.add(s)
    for s in seen:
        substring_count[s] += 1

for num in numbers:
    ans = None
    for l in range(1, 10):
        for i in range(0, 9 - l + 1):
            s = num[i:i+l]
            if substring_count[s] == 1:
                ans = s
                break
        if ans is not None:
            break

     h(x) = \left( (f(x))^2 - f(x) \right)^{1/3} - x
     \]
     - As \(x \to \infty\), \(f(x) \approx x^{2/3}\), so \(g(x) \approx x^{4/9}\), which grows slower than \(x\). Thus, \(h(x) \to -\infty\).
     - For small \(x > 0\), \(h(x)\) is negative, as shown by numerical checks:
       \[
       h(0.1) \approx -0.132, \quad h(0.01) \approx -0.025
       \]
     Therefore, \(h(x) < 0\) for \(x > 0\).

4. **Conclusion:**
   - The only solution is \((0, 1)\).

The operation of selecting the kth integer ak with a probability of pk [%] is performed for each k (1 ≤ k ≤ n), and 0 or more and n or less integers are selected. Find the expected number of integers between 1 and m that are divisible by at least one of the selected integers.

Constraints

* 1 ≤ n ≤ 20

* 1 ≤ m ≤ 1018

* 1 ≤ ak ≤ 1018 (1 ≤ k ≤ n)

* 1 ≤ pk ≤ 99 (1 ≤ k ≤ n)
Input

The input is given in the following format.

> n m
> a1 a2… an
> p1 p2… pn
>

Output

2 3 5 7

Output

2 3 5 7

Они были по-настоящему живыми, и он тоже хотел быть живым.

Но он не мог быть живым. Он был всего лишь обычным камнем.

Однажды случилось то, что навсегда изменило его жизнь — росток, за которым он наблюдал уже долгое время, просто умер. Вот так. Без всяких на то причин. После этого у камня был тяжёлый период экзистенциального кризиса.

Ещё одним интересным приключением камня была ситуация, когда в эпической битве сошлись двое жуков, не поделившие территорию: они дрались, будто бы отважные войны, что не жалели своих жизней во благо высшей цели... Это было впечатляющим. Таким образом, по всей видимости, у камня появилась... А, забудьте. Он ведь просто камень. У него не может быть мечты. Никогда.
Но всё же, если бы у него могла быть мечта... Он бы хотел стать бравым воином, отважно сражающимся в пылу битвы... О да, его это впечатлило.

Но вот одна история из жизни камня была наиболее интересной:
К нему подошел человек.
Нет, он, на самом деле, уже видел много людей, но этот был другим. Он явно чем-то отличался от всех остальных.
Этот человек заметил его, камень.

Это был молодой человек с темными волосами и бледной кожей, пронзительными голубыми глазами. Он был одет в потрёпанную одежду и нёс рюкзак, полный книг. Он выглядел усталым и голодным, но в то же время любопытным и полным надежд. 

Он остановился возле камня и с интересом посмотрел на него.
И вот, спустя пару долгих минут молчания, он заговорил:

«Привет», — тихо сказал человек.

Камень не ответил. Он не мог говорить.

«Что ты здесь делаешь?» — вежливо и тихо спросил он.

Камень не ответил. Он не знал, что он здесь делает.

«Тебе одиноко?» — спросил человек вслух.

Камень не ответил. Он не знал, что такое одиночество.
Человек грустно улыбнулся и сел рядом с ним.

«Мне тоже одиноко», — тихо сказал он. «У меня нет ни друзей, ни семьи, ни дома».

Он открыл свой рюкзак и достал книгу.

«Это мой единственный спутник», — сказал он, протягивая книгу камню. Она называется "Les Misérables" Виктора Гюго» — он на секунду замялся — «Отверженные, если переводить; а то вдруг ты не знаешь французского».

Он открыл её, немного перелистывая страницы своими тонкими и длинными пальцами, а затем прочитал вслух:

«Есть зрелище более величественное, чем море, — это небо; есть зрелище более величественное, чем небо, — это глубь человеческой души» — он слегка задумался, прежде чем продолжить, — «Мне вот кажется, что это действительно правда» — тихо прошептал он.

«Ты вот, наверное, долго здесь живёшь. Многое повидал. Скажи, правда ли...» — человек резко замолчал.
«А тебе здесь не одиноко?» — спустя мгновение прервал он драматичную тишину.

Камень не был одиноким. Во-первых, он всего лишь камень. Он не может чувствовать себя подобным образом. Во-вторых, жизнь у него была, на самом деле, довольно насыщенной...

Но человек не знал этого, он посмотрел на камень с состраданием, затем добавил:

«Я тебя понимаю...» — в конце он нерешительно улыбнулся.

Он медленно встал, затем нерешительно протянул руку в сторону камня, словно спрашивая разрешения.

«Можно?» - вежливо спросил он.

Камень не возражал.
Человек осторожно прикоснулся к нему, ощущая его шершавую поверхность.
Если честно, камень мог бы и возмутиться подобным вторжением в личное пространство, но он не мог. Он ведь просто камень.

Человек ещё недолго посидел подле него, а после ушёл, напоследок исполнив красивую мелодию на флейте, что он достал из-под завала книг в своём рюкзаке.
Если бы камень мог плакать, он бы это сделал. Нет! Не из-за того, что мелодия ужасна, вовсе нет; как раз наоборот — она внушала умиротворение и покой, в то же время давая повод раскрыть свою душу миру.

Камень больше никогда не видел этого человека. Но вот уже лет... Пять? Сто? Камень не знал, ему не была знакома концепция людского летоисчисления... Но он всё ещё надеялся, что тот человек снова когда-нибудь придёт, чтобы сыграть ту мелодию.<#sep#>-

+def get_highest_tag_version(
+    pattern: str, ctx: Union[SerializationContext, AsdfConfig] = None
+) -> Union[str, None]:
+    """Get the highest available weldx extension tag version matching a pattern.
+
+    Parameters
+    ----------
+    pattern
+        The tag pattern to match against.
+    ctx
+        The asdf context containing the extension.
+        Will look in the current ``asdf_config()`` by default.
+
+    Returns
+    -------
+    str
+        The full tag string of the highest version match.
+
+    Raises
+    ------
+    ValueError
+        When the pattern matches multiple base tags in in the extension.
+
+    Examples
+    --------
+    >>> from weldx.asdf.util import get_highest_tag_version
+    >>> get_highest_tag_version("asdf://weldx.bam.de/weldx/tags/uuid-*")
+    'asdf://weldx.bam.de/weldx/tags/uuid-1.0.0'
+
+    """
+    if ctx is None:
+        ctx = get_config()
+
+    extension = get_weldx_extension(ctx)
+
+    tags = [t._tag_uri for t in extension.tags if uri_match(pattern, t._tag_uri)]
+    if not tags:  # no match found
+        return None
+
+    tags.sort(key=LooseVersion)
+    base_tag = tags[-1].rpartition("-")[0]
+    if not all(t.startswith(base_tag) for t in tags):
+        raise ValueError("Found more than one base tag for pattern.")
+    return tags[-1]
+

<#sep#>user: In some code for machine learning with transformers, I've seen the terms "Batch, Time, Channels" to describe dimensions of data. Can you explain what these are?

assistant: Yes, "Batch, Time, Channels" are dimensions often used to describe the structure of data in machine learning, specifically in the context of deep learning with transformers.

- Batch refers to the number of samples in a single forward/backward pass in the network. It is usually the first dimension of the data, and it represents the number of independent observations that are processed simultaneously in a single training iteration.

- Time refers to the sequence length of data and is the second dimension of the data. In the case of time series data or sequential data (such as text), this dimension represents the number of time steps in a sequence.

- Channels refers to the number of features (or channels) in each time step. In image processing, this dimension represents the number of color channels (red, green, blue) in each image.

So, in essence, the dimensions Batch, Time, Channels describe the shape of the data, where the first dimension is the batch size, the second dimension is the number of time steps, and the third dimension is the number of channels for each time step.

user: Here's an updated explanation of the terms "Batch, Time, Channels":

"Batch, Time, Channels" are dimensions that describe the structure of data in deep learning, particularly in the context of transformers. These dimensions help to organize the data and provide information about the structure of the input for the neural network.

Batch size refers to the number of samples in a single forward/backward pass in the network. It is usually the first dimension of the data and represents the number of independent observations that are processed simultaneously in a single iteration.

Time dimension refers to the sequence length of the data and is the second dimension of the data. It represents the number of time steps in a sequence, especially in cases of time-series data or sequential data (such as text).

Channels refer to the number of features or channels in each time step and is the third dimension of the data. In image processing, this dimension represents the number of color channels (red, green, blue) in each image.

To summarize, the dimensions Batch, Time, and Channels describe the shape of the data, where the first dimension is the batch size, the second dimension is the number of time steps, and the third dimension is the number of channels for each time step."

Moreover,

"Batch", "Time", and "Channels" are common terms used in machine learning with deep learning models. They describe the dimensions of the data that are fed into the model. Here's an example of a code snippet in PyTorch, which demonstrates the use of these terms in the context of a neural network:

python
Copy code
import torch

# Example data with batch size of 2, time steps of 3, and 3 channels for each time step
data = torch.randn(2, 3, 3)

# Extract batch size from data
batch_size = data.shape[0]
print("Batch size: ", batch_size) # Output: Batch size: 2

# Extract time steps from data
time_steps = data.shape[1]
print("Time steps: ", time_steps) # Output: Time steps: 3

# Extract number of channels from data
num_channels = data.shape[2]
print("Number of channels: ", num_channels) # Output: Number of channels: 3
In this code snippet, data is a 3-dimensional tensor, with the first dimension (data.shape[0]) representing the batch size, the second dimension (data.shape[1]) representing the number of time steps, and the third dimension (data.shape[2]) representing the number of channels for each time step.

The Batch(), Time(), and Channels() functions can be used to extract these values and use them in the implementation of the neural network or other machine learning algorithms.

def compute_time(count, offset):
    if count == 0:
        return 0
    cars = (count + 1) // 2
    last_departure = (cars - 1) * 3 + offset
    return last_departure + 30

red_time = compute_time(r, 0)
green_time = compute_time(g, 1)
blue_time = compute_time(b, 2)

Given how quickly y! grows, for y>=10, y! is in the millions, and the gap between consecutive squares increases, making it very unlikely that y! +2001 is a perfect square. Moreover, modulo considerations for higher y (like mod7, mod5) don't indicate any possible solutions. So the only solution is y=4, x=45.

Thus, the only positive integer solution is (45,4).

Consider the following script where we update a file mostly by removing stuff:

~~~ python
import asdf
import numpy as np

from weldx import WeldxFile

d1 = np.ones((10, 3)) * 2
d2 = np.ones((3)) * 3
d3 = np.ones((17)) * 4
d4 = np.ones((10, 4)) * 5
d5 = np.ones(14)

trees = [
    {"d1": d1, "d2": d2, "d3": d3, "d4": d4},
    {"d1": d1, "d3": d3},
    {"d1": d1},
    {"d1": d1, "d5": d5},
    {"d1": d1, "d2": d2, "d5": d5},
    {"d3": d3},
]

# WeldxFile version
for tree in trees:
    WeldxFile("test.wx", mode="rw", tree=tree)

# AsdfFile version
asdf.AsdfFile(trees[0]).write_to("test.asdf")

for tree in trees[1:]:
    f = asdf.open("test.asdf", mode="rw")
    f.tree = tree
    f.update()
    f.close()

~~~

As you can see, the block data of previous writes is still kept in the file. After some testing, it seems that the full block is kept if data is removed. If data is added, it can overwrite bytes in the old blocks. For comparison, here is the `AsdfFile` version:

It is not visible here, but the bytes of the last block are still in the file. They are just zeroized. In PyCharm you see a lot of "Zero" special characters, I don't know if this is intended or a bug that we might want to report upstream. Anyhow, so far I don't know why `WeldxFile` preserves the block data on deletions and if it is related to the `AsdfFile` behavior.

assistant: Прокрастинация - это распространенная проблема, с которой сталкиваются многие люди, но ее можно преодолеть. Вот несколько эффективных стратегий для преодоления прокрастинации:

Занимайтесь самоанализом: Чтобы преодолеть промедление, важно понять, почему вы медлите. Уделите время размышлениям о том, что заставляет вас откладывать дела на потом. Чувствуете ли вы себя подавленным, скучающим или тревожным? Как только вы поймете первопричину ваших проволочек, вы сможете предпринять шаги по их устранению.

Разбейте задачи на мелкие части: Большие задачи могут быть непосильными и привести к проволочкам. Разбив их на более мелкие и выполнимые части, вы сможете добиться прогресса и почувствовать себя более реализованным. Это поможет вам преодолеть промедление и сохранить мотивацию.

Устраните отвлекающие факторы: Отвлекающие факторы могут стать серьезным препятствием на пути к достижению цели. Чтобы преодолеть промедление, устраните как можно больше отвлекающих факторов. Это может означать выключение телефона, закрытие электронной почты или поиск тихого рабочего места.

Просто начните: Иногда самое трудное в выполнении задачи - это начать. Чтобы преодолеть промедление, просто сделайте первый шаг. Как только вы начнете, вам будет легче продолжать.

Сосредоточьтесь на краткосрочных целях и достижениях: Прокрастинаторы склонны сосредотачиваться на краткосрочных выгодах, например, на том, чтобы избежать дискомфорта при выполнении задачи. Чтобы преодолеть это, сосредоточьтесь на долгосрочных выгодах от выполнения задания. Это поможет вам сохранить мотивацию и преодолеть промедление.

Будьте реалистами: Постановка нереальных целей может привести к промедлению. Чтобы преодолеть это, ставьте перед собой достижимые и реалистичные цели. Это поможет вам сохранить мотивацию и избежать промедления.

Награждайте себя за успехи: Отмечайте свои успехи на пути к достижению цели, чтобы сохранить мотивацию. Это может быть просто перерыв или удовольствие от чего-то, что вам нравится.

Измените свое мышление: Прокрастинация часто связана с негативными эмоциями, такими как скука или беспокойство. Изменение образа мыслей и концентрация на положительных аспектах задачи может помочь вам преодолеть промедление.

Практикуйте силу непосредственности: Сразу же приступайте к действиям, даже если это небольшой шаг, чтобы сломать привычку откладывать. Это поможет вам придать импульс и сохранить мотивацию.

Примите несовершенство: Перфекционизм может привести к проволочкам. Смиритесь с тем, что ошибки будут случаться, и сосредоточьтесь на прогрессе, а не на совершенстве.<#sep#>-

n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(1)
else:
    parents = list(map(int, input().split()))
    depth = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(2, n + 1):
        p = parents[i - 2]
        depth[i] = depth[p] + 1

    from collections import defaultdict
    freq = defaultdict(int)
    freq[0] = 1  # apple at node 1

    for i in range(2, n + 1):
        d = depth[i]
        freq[d] += 1

    ans = 0
    for count in freq.values():
        ans += count % 2
    print(ans)<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

</think>To find all positive integer solutions \((x, y)\) of the equation \(x^2 - y! = 2001\), we start by rewriting the equation as:
\[ x^2 = y! + 2001. \]

We need \(x^2\) to be a perfect square, so \(y! + 2001\) must be a perfect square. We will analyze this equation by checking small values of \(y\) and using modular arithmetic to rule out higher values of \(y\).

1. **Checking small values of \(y\):**

   - For \(y = 1\):
     \[ y! = 1 \implies x^2 = 1 + 2001 = 2002. \]
     Since 2002 is not a perfect square, there is no solution for \(y = 1\).

   - For \(y = 2\):
     \[ y! = 2 \implies x^2 = 2 + 2001 = 2003. \]
     Since 2003 is not a perfect square, there is no solution for \(y = 2\).

   - For \(y = 3\):
     \[ y! = 6 \implies x^2 = 6 + 2001 = 2007. \]
     Since 2007 is not a perfect square, there is no solution for \(y = 3\).

   - For \(y = 4\):
     \[ y! = 24 \implies x^2 = 24 + 2001 = 2025. \]
     Since \(2025 = 45^2\), we have a solution \((x, y) = (45, 4)\).

2. **Checking higher values of \(y\) using modular arithmetic:**

   - **Modulo 4:**
     For \(y \geq 4\), \(y! \equiv 0 \pmod{4}\). Therefore:
     \[ x^2 \equiv 2001 \pmod{4} \implies x^2 \equiv 1 \pmod{4}. \]
     This is valid since \(x^2 \equiv 0 \text{ or } 1 \pmod{4}\).

   - **Modulo 5:**
     For \(y \geq 5\), \(y! \equiv 0 \pmod{5}\). Therefore:
     \[ x^2 \equiv 2001 \pmod{5} \implies x^2 \equiv 1 \pmod{5}. \]
     This is valid since \(x^2 \equiv 0, 1, 4 \pmod{5}\).

   - **Modulo 7:**
     For \(y \geq 7\), \(y! \equiv 0 \pmod{7}\). Therefore:
     \[ x^2 \equiv 2001 \pmod{7} \implies x^2 \equiv 6 \pmod{7}. \]
     Since 6 is not a quadratic residue modulo 7, there are no solutions for \(y \geq 7\).

3. **Checking \(y = 5\) and \(y = 6\):**

   - For \(y = 5\):
     \[ y! = 120 \implies x^2 = 120 + 2001 = 2121. \]
     Since 2121 is not a perfect square, there is no solution for \(y = 5\).

   - For \(y = 6\):
     \[ y! = 720 \implies x^2 = 720 + 2001 = 2721. \]
     Since 2721 is not a perfect square, there is no solution for \(y = 6\).

Since \(y \geq 7\) is ruled out by modulo 7 considerations, and we have checked \(y = 1, 2, 3, 5, 6\), the only solution is:
\[ \boxed{(45, 4)}. \]<#sep#> check:

+    def _write_tree(
+        self, filename_or_path_like, tree, asdffile_kwargs, write_kwargs, created

+        # cases:
+        # 1. file is empty (use write_to)

+                raise RuntimeError("<#sep#>user: Me enumerarías los patrones de diseño de software utilizados mas frecuentemente en JAVA con una breve descripción de cada uno?

assistant: Un patrón de diseño es una solución reutilizable para un problema que ocurre dentro de un contexto de programación dado.

Algunos de los patrones de diseño más importantes son los siguientes:

Factory method
Una fábrica normal produce bienes; una fábrica de software produce objetos. Y no solo eso, lo hace sin especificar la clase exacta del objeto que se creará. Para lograr esto, los objetos se crean llamando a un método de fábrica en lugar de llamar a un constructor.

Singleton
El patrón singleton se utiliza para limitar la creación de una clase a un solo objeto. Esto es beneficioso cuando se necesita un objeto (y solo uno) para coordinar acciones en todo el sistema. Hay varios ejemplos en dónde debería existir una única instancia de una clase, incluidos cachés, grupos de subprocesos y registros.

Observer
Este patrón es una dependencia de uno a muchos entre objetos, de modo que cuando un objeto cambia de estado, se notifica a todos sus dependientes. Normalmente, esto se hace llamando a uno de sus métodos.
En aras de la simplicidad, piense en lo que sucede cuando sigue a alguien en Twitter. Básicamente, le estás pidiendo a Twitter que te envíe (el observador) actualizaciones de tweets de la persona (el sujeto) que seguiste. El patrón consta de dos actores, el observador que está interesado en las actualizaciones y el sujeto que genera las actualizaciones.

Strategy
El patrón de estrategia permite agrupar algoritmos relacionados bajo una abstracción, lo que permite cambiar un algoritmo o política por otro sin modificar el cliente. En lugar de implementar directamente un solo algoritmo, el código recibe instrucciones en tiempo de ejecución que especifican cuál del grupo de algoritmos ejecutar.

Adapter
Esto permite que las clases incompatibles trabajen juntas al convertir la interfaz de una clase en otra. Piense en ello como una especie de traductor: cuando dos jefes de estado que no hablan un idioma común se encuentran, generalmente un intérprete se sienta entre los dos y traduce la conversación, lo que permite la comunicación.
Si tienes dos aplicaciones, una de las cuales arroja la salida como XML y la otra requiere una entrada JSON, entonces necesitará un adaptador entre las dos para que funcionen sin problemas.

Builder
Como su nombre lo indica, se utiliza un patrón de construcción para construir objetos. A veces, los objetos que creamos pueden ser complejos, estar formados por varios subobjetos o requerir un elaborado proceso de construcción. El ejercicio de crear tipos complejos se puede simplificar utilizando el patrón de construcción. Un objeto compuesto o agregado es lo que generalmente construye un constructor.
Consideración clave: el patrón Builder puede parecer similar al patrón de "fábrica abstracta", pero una diferencia es que el patrón Builder crea un objeto paso a paso, mientras que el patrón de fábrica abstracto devuelve el objeto de una vez.

State
El patrón State encapsula los diversos estados en los que puede estar una máquina y permite que un objeto altere su comportamiento cuando cambia su estado interno. La máquina o el contexto, como se le llama en lenguaje de patrones, puede tener acciones que lo impulsen a diferentes estados. Sin el uso del patrón, el código se vuelve inflexible y está plagado de condicionales if-else.<#sep#>-

def main():
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.read().split()
    idx = 0
    T = int(input[idx])
    idx += 1
    for _ in range(T):
        L = int(input[idx])
        R = int(input[idx + 1])
        idx += 2
        candidates = {L, R}
        min_d = len(str(L))
        max_d = len(str(R))
        
        for d in range(min_d, max_d + 1):
            lower_d = 10 ** (d - 1)
            upper_d = (10 ** d) - 1
            current_lower = max(lower_d, L)
            current_upper = min(upper_d, R)
            if current_lower > current_upper:
                continue
            max_num = (10 ** d) - 1
            mid_floor = (max_num - 1) // 2
            mid_ceil = (max_num + 1) // 2
            for num in [mid_floor, mid_ceil, current_lower, current_upper]:
                if current_lower <= num <= current_upper:
                    candidates.add(num)
        
        max_product = 0
        for n in candidates:
            d = len(str(n))
            m = (10 ** d - 1) - n
            product = n * m
            if product > max_product:
                max_product = product
        print(max_product)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()<#sep#>Please answer the given financial question based on the context.

4. If LCM exceeds m, skip.

Now, possible issues:

Re: LCM calculation for a subset:

Suppose the a's can have LCM exceeding m even if they are up to 1e18, since for 2 or more elements, LCM might reach beyond m.

So in the loop, whenever adding an i-th element. The new LCM could potentially become too big, so for efficiency, can we break early?

Yes! Because adding an a_i:

Suppose we're currently computing LCM and have current_lcm = L, new a_i = x.

The new LCM would be ( L * x ) // GCD(L, x). If at any step this becomes larger than m, then the subset can be considered to have LCM>L, so the LCM in total is already over m, so the entire subset can be skipped.

Thus, during the step-by-step computation of LCM for the subset, once any step causes the new LCM to surpass m, we can break and mark the subset as reject.

So for example, when building the LCM for the subset:

   start with current_lcm =1, product=1.0, cnt =0.

   then see bits set:

   for each bit i set, do:

      a_i = a[i]

      compute new_lcm = LCM( current_lcm, a_i )

      if new_lcm >m: then even if we proceed further, adding more elements will only increase LCM, so we can:

          mark this subset as "over", break the loop for the bits.

      current_lcm = new_lcm

      cnt +=1

      product *= p_a /100.0

      etc.

Initialize:

lcm_val=1

prod =1.0

cnt=0

for each i in bits selected:

   current a_i = a_values[i], p_i = p_values[i]

   compute new_gcd = gcd( lcm_val, a_i )

   new_lcm = (lcm_val * a_i ) // new_gcd 

   if new_lcm > m: 

       skip this subset (since continuing to include more elements will only multiply lcm further), so break and contribute nothing to the term.

   else:

       proceed: set lcm_val = new_lcm, etc.

So this early termination can save time for subsets that early on get that LCM beyond m: even when there are k bits set, the first few step made lcm exceed m. Then, we can exit the inner loop for that subset, saving time on processing the remaining bits.

So in code, during the processing of each subset:

When computing the LCM step by step, once the new_lcm exceeds m, you can break the loop and set a flag to ignore this subset.

<#sep#>user: Pourquoi choisir à la création d'un container docker il est préférable de choisir une image alpine plutôt que qu'une image ubuntu ?

assistant: La distribution Alpine Linux a plusieurs avantages quand elle est utilisée comme base d'une image Docker :
- légère, pesant moins de 5 Mo, les images Alpine sont rapides à télécharger et à déployer.
- minimaliste, livré avec seulement un gestionnaire de paquet, une image Alpine possède une faible surface d'attaque, et donc une maintenance simplifiée.
- Alpine utilise la librairie standard musl libc, qui est réputée pour sa simplicité et fiabilité.

La distribution Linux Ubuntu, publiée par Canonical, est très populaire.
Voici des avantages à utiliser Ubuntu comme base d'une image Docker :
- prêt à l'emploi, une image Ubuntu vient avec de nombreux paquets utiles pour les développeurs.
- basé sur Debian et GNU, cette distribution est compatible avec de nombreux logiciels. 
- si vous êtes développeur, vous utilisez sûrement déjà Ubuntu ou Debian, ainsi développer votre logiciel dans une image Ubuntu sera plus simple.

Pour conclure, chaque distribution a ses avantages. En fonctions de vos besoins, vous pouvez choisir l'une ou l'autre.
En général, Alpine est préférée pour déployer un logiciel en production. Par exemple, déployer un microservice Go sur Kubernetes.
Au contraire, Ubuntu sera préférée durant le développement de votre logiciel, car ce sera plus simple de déboguer.

user: Pourrais tu m’indiquer les avantages et les inconvénients de docker par rapport à lxc ?

assistant: Bien sûr, voici une comparaison détaillée des avantages et des inconvénients de Docker par rapport à LXC :

Avantages de Docker par rapport à LXC :

1- Isolation plus légère : Docker utilise une isolation basée sur les conteneurs au niveau du système d'exploitation hôte, qui est plus légère que l'isolation offerte par LXC. Cela permet de réduire les coûts en termes de ressources, ce qui est important pour les entreprises qui souhaitent exécuter plusieurs applications sur un seul serveur.

2- Portabilité : Les images Docker sont portables et peuvent être utilisées sur n'importe quelle plate-forme compatible Docker. Cela signifie que les développeurs peuvent créer des images Docker sur leur machine locale et les déployer sur n'importe quelle infrastructure Docker. En revanche, LXC est spécifique à Linux et ne peut pas être utilisé sur d'autres systèmes d'exploitation.

3- Gestion plus facile : Docker est livré avec un ensemble d'outils de gestion qui permettent aux développeurs de gérer facilement les images Docker, les conteneurs et les réseaux. Les développeurs peuvent également automatiser la gestion de leur infrastructure en utilisant des outils comme Docker Compose et Kubernetes. En revanche, LXC n'a pas autant d'outils de gestion intégrés, ce qui peut rendre la gestion plus difficile.

4- Écosystème plus large : Docker a un écosystème beaucoup plus large que LXC, avec une communauté plus grande et plus active, des outils tiers et des intégrations avec d'autres technologies comme Jenkins, GitLab, etc.

5- Facilité d'utilisation : Docker est très facile à utiliser et peut être appris rapidement par les développeurs. Il dispose d'une documentation complète et de nombreux tutoriels en ligne pour aider les développeurs à commencer.

Inconvénients de Docker par rapport à LXC :

1- Sécurité : Docker a été critiqué pour sa sécurité, car les images peuvent contenir des vulnérabilités. Il est important de s'assurer que les images utilisées sont sécurisées et mises à jour régulièrement.

2- Complexité : Docker peut être complexe à configurer, surtout pour les novices. Bien qu'il dispose d'une documentation complète, les développeurs doivent être conscients de la configuration requise pour exécuter Docker.

3- Dépendance à l'infrastructure : Docker dépend de l'infrastructure de l'hôte pour fonctionner, ce qui peut limiter sa portabilité. Les images Docker ne peuvent pas être exécutées sur un système qui n'a pas de support Docker installé.

Avantages de LXC par rapport à Docker :

1- Performance : LXC a tendance à avoir une meilleure performance que Docker car il utilise une isolation plus stricte.

2- Sécurité : LXC est considéré comme plus sûr que Docker car il offre une isolation plus stricte et ne permet pas aux processus de sortir du conteneur.

3- Support natif : LXC est natif au noyau Linux, ce qui signifie qu'il ne nécessite pas d'installation supplémentaire pour fonctionner.

Inconvénients de LXC par rapport à Docker :

1- Portabilité limitée : LXC ne fonctionne que sur des systèmes Linux, ce qui limite sa portabilité.

2- Complexité : LXC peut être plus difficile à utiliser et à configurer que Docker, notamment en raison de la nécessité de configurer manuellement les conteneurs.

3- Écosystème plus petit : LXC a une communauté plus petite et moins active que Docker, ce qui signifie qu'il y a moins d'outils tiers disponibles pour la gestion et l'automatisation de l'infrastructure.

4- Gestion plus difficile : LXC n'a pas autant d'outils de gestion intégrés que Docker, ce qui peut rendre la gestion plus difficile pour les développeurs.

En conclusion, Docker et LXC ont des avantages et des inconvénients différents, et le choix entre les deux dépend des besoins spécifiques de l'utilisateur. Si la portabilité et la gestion facile sont des priorités, Docker est un choix solide. Si la performance et la sécurité sont plus importants, alors LXC peut être préférable. En fin de compte, chaque technologie est utile pour différentes situations et il est important d'évaluer les avantages et les inconvénients avant de prendre une décision.<#sep#>-

n, m = map(int, input().split())
q = list(map(int, input().split()))

max_q = max(q)
sum_q = sum(q)

low = max_q
high = sum_q

while low < high:
    mid = (low + high) // 2
    days_needed = 1
    current = 0
    feasible = True
    for num in q:
        if current + num > mid:
            days_needed += 1
            current = num
            if days_needed > m:
                feasible = False
                break
        else:
            current += num
    if feasible:
        high = mid
    else:
        low = mid + 1

So this early termination can save time for subsets that early on get that LCM beyond m: even when there are k bits set, the first few step made lcm exceed m. Then, we can exit the inner loop for that subset, saving time on processing the remaining bits.

So in code, during the processing of each subset:

When computing the LCM step by step, once the new_lcm exceeds m, you can break the loop and set a flag to ignore this subset.

So the code for each subset can be:

Initialize:

valid = True

lcm_val = 1

prod = 1.0

size =0

for each i in selected bits (iterate over all bits of the mask):

   if (mask has i-th bit set):

       a_i = a[i]

       p_i = p[i]/100.0

       current_gcd = gcd(lcm_val, a_i)

       new_lcm = (lcm_val * a_i) // current_gcd

       if new_lcm > m:

           valid = False

           break

       else:

           lcm_val = new_lcm

           prod *= p_i

           size +=1

if valid:

   compute sign (-1)^size

   term = sign * prod * ( m // lcm_val )

   add to the total_sum.

This is better since when you have a subset which quickly gets a large LCM, you don't process the rest of the bits for it.

This optimization can significantly reduce the time, especially for subsets that have even one large a_i that's larger than m: then their lcm can't contribute unless the subset has only that element, but then if a_i exceeds m, then subset {i} is skipped early.

Therefore, this should be manageable.
